Let me think about the proof strategy carefully.

We have STLC with CBV semantics, locally nameless. The target theorem: closed well-typed term is SN.

Key challenges:
1. The step relation's ST_App1/ST_App2/ST_If constructors require `locally_closed` premises — those are extra premises but we shouldn't need to use them much.
2. We need a logical relation. The standard approach: define `sem T t` (unary logical relation) with:
   - `sem Bool t := strongly_normalizing t` (or maybe `SN t ∧ (t value → t = true ∨ t = false)`? For proving fundamental theorem we need enough.)
   - `sem (T1 -> T2) t := SN t ∧ forall u, sem T1 u -> SN (tm_app t u) ∧ sem T2 (tm_app t u)` — actually for CBV, the standard LR: `sem (T1->T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, sem T1 u -> sem T2 (app t u)`.

But the fundamental theorem works with open terms under a context interpretation. The tricky bit is locally nameless: the typing judgment uses `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` for abstractions. We need substitution/opening lemmas: `open_rec` on `tm_fvar x` etc.

Actually a cleaner approach: use the "semantic typing" via context interpretation on free variables, i.e., define `sem_ctx Gamma` as a relation on (atom -> tm) substitutions? But the language here doesn't have explicit substitution; opening substitutes for bvar 0.

Hmm. The typing rule T_Abs requires: for all x not in L, `x |-> T1; Gamma |-- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2`. So in the fundamental theorem for lambda, we get sem for `open t1 (fvar x)` in context `x |-> T1 ; Gamma`. To conclude sem (T1->T2) (lambda T1 t1), we need: SN (abs T1 t1) — easy from value + body closed? Actually SN of a value requires all its reducts SN, and abs has no reducts unless applied, so SN (abs T1 t1) holds trivially (no step from a bare value). Indeed `tm_abs` alone doesn't step. So SN (abs T1 t1) is easy.

Then we need for all u with sem T1 u: sem T2 (app (abs T1 t1) u). Via the LR for arrow, we would need to show: app (abs T1 t1) u steps to open t1 u, and open t1 u is SN. Hmm, we know sem T2 (open t1 (fvar x)) for fresh x, with x being a variable whose value we can substitute by u. So we need a "substitution lemma" for the LR: if sem under context mapping x to sem-value, then replacing fvar x by the actual term u preserves semantics. This is the standard approach: prove the fundamental theorem with a semantic context `Gamma` interpreted as an assignment from atoms to terms satisfying the LR. But the typing judgment happens with contexts, and the "opening with fvar x" is exactly the variable case.

Alternative standard approach (TAPL-style, "Adequacy" using `lc` and substitution): Define LR and prove:
- Fundamental theorem: for all Gamma t T, Gamma |-- t \in T -> for all sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> sem T (sigma x)) -> sem T (t with fvar x replaced by sigma x)... but here we don't have substitution for fvars directly; the language has fvars as atoms and open_rec substitutes bvars. There is no general fvar-substitution function. But we can define one ourselves: `subst_fvar (sigma : atom -> tm) t`. That's fine — we may add definitions.

Then handle T_Abs case: `Gamma |-- lambda T1 t1 \in T1 -> T2`. We need sem (T1 -> T2) (lambda T1 t1) under sigma. Requirements: SN (lambda T1 t1) (easy: it's a value, no steps... wait, `step` from `tm_abs` alone: no constructor applies, since step constructors all have app or if at top. So no steps → SN via SN_intro with no premises). Then for u with sem T1 u, show sem T2 (app (lambda T1 t1) u). We know app (lambda T1 t1) u --> open t1 u, provided locally_closed (abs T1 t1) and value u. Hmm, the step requires locally_closed (lambda T1 t1) and value u. Do we have those? In a closed-well-typed-term setting, terms are locally closed, but the fundamental theorem needs it as a hypothesis. Hmm.

This is the key difficulty: the step relation requires `locally_closed` premises on ST_AppAbs. For SN purposes, we need SN (app (lambda T1 t1) u) given SN of the reduct. SN is contravariant along step? Actually to show SN t, we need all t' with t --> t' are SN. So we need: for all t' with app (abs) u --> t', SN t'. The possible reducts: open t1 u (via ST_AppAbs if lc and value u), and app (abs) u' where u --> u' (via ST_App2, needs value (abs) — we have that since... value requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t), hmm! value (tm_abs T t) requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t)).

So value is defined with a locally_closed premise. This means to use value of an abstraction we need local closure. And ST_App2 requires value v1. So to prove everything we likely need to know terms are locally closed. Hmm. Well, in a well-typed term context, are all terms locally closed? Yes, standard: well-typed terms are locally closed. But we'd need to prove that (requires a lemma about open_rec/lc_at and fvars). Alternatively, carry local-closure through the logical relation: strengthen the LR to include "lc" or combine SN with something.

Alternative approach: strengthen LR as: `sem T t := SN t ∧ locally_closed t`? Hmm, but for a Bool type with a term like `fvar x`, locally closed holds trivially (lc_fvar). Fine.

But there's another subtlety: for the arrow case, the LR needs SN t and for all u sem T1 u, sem T2 (app t u). This requires app t u to be SN. If t is an abstraction and u is a value, app steps to open t u. We need the LR substitution lemma to conclude sem T2 (open t1 u) from sem T2 (open t1 (fvar x)).

Hmm, the typical way in locally nameless developments (e.g., the "locally nameless" tutorial / Software Foundations' Stlc with LN) is:

For strong normalization with LN, it's often easier to define LR on closed terms only, then show fundamental theorem for terms in context via a context of "free variable substitutions" and prove "well-typed terms are locally closed".

Actually, an easier approach for this benchmark: Maybe we can avoid the substitution lemma by defining the logical relation over the *open* form directly, and use a `sem_ctx` interpretation: Define `sem T t` for arbitrary t, and prove:
1. Fundamental theorem generalized: for all Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> for all sigma : atom -> tm, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> sem T (subst sigma t) where sem_ctx Gamma sigma := forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> sem T (sigma x).

Here `subst sigma` replaces free variables by sigma. We define `subst_fvar`.

Hmm wait, but in the T_Abs case, we need to relate `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` with `open (subst?) ...`. The body in the conclusion is `lambda T1 t1`, and we need sem (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 t1) [sigma-substituted: subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1) = tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1) since substitution doesn't touch bvars... but careful: open_rec substitutes bvar 0 and sigma substitutes fvars; they commute nicely].

We know from the IH: for fresh x not in L (and also not in the free vars of anything relevant), sem T2 (subst (x |-> u ; sigma) (open t1 (fvar x))).

Now subst (x|->u;sigma) (open t1 (fvar x)) — is this equal to open (subst sigma t1) u? If x is fresh (not free in t1) and u is a closed term... hmm, u need not be closed; u may contain fvars? In our setting, terms in the LR may contain fvars? We'd need to know that substitution behaves. This is the standard "substitution lemma" for opening.

This is getting complicated. Alternative much simpler approach: since the theorem only concerns *closed* terms typed in the empty context, maybe we can first prove a standard "closure" lemma: well-typed terms are locally closed, and then work with a simpler LR defined on locally closed terms? Hmm, but the fundamental theorem still needs context handling.

Alternative approach avoiding substitution lemmas entirely: Use the "syntactic" approach: define a *reduction-based* criterion. E.g., prove normalization by the standard Tait-style with a different formulation: Define for each type T, a predicate on terms; use the fact that typing is closed. But the T_Abs case fundamentally requires relating body with a variable to body with arbitrary u — that's substitution.

There's no way around substitution, but we can choose the cleanest formulation.

Let me consider the well-known approach from Software Foundations' `StlcProp` / or the "locally nameless" style used in the LN tutorial (`LC` chapter of SF "UseTactics"?). Actually there's a known benchmark family ("STLC strong normalization") from the "Logical Relations" exercises, e.g., from `pl-agda` or from "Software Foundations volume 2, chapter Norm". In SF, `norm` chapter uses `normal_form` and a "strongly normalizing" definition similar to this.

Approach with fvar substitution:
- Define `subst_fvar (s : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm` recursively, substituting tm_fvar x with s x, leaving bvars.
- Define `subst_open`: substituting a term for fvar x commutes with open.

Hmm. Let's think about what's actually needed:

LR definition (for closed-ish terms):
```
Definition LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u)
  end.
```
Then fundamental theorem: for all Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x)) -> LR T (subst sigma t).

For T_Abs: sigma arbitrary, show LR (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) [note subst doesn't recurse under binder for bvars, so subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1) = tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)].

Parts: SN (abs ...) — need no steps out of it. Fine: SN_intro with intros t' Hstep; destruct Hstep... Actually we need `forall t', tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1) --> t' -> SN t'`, and there are no such t' (all step constructors produce app or if as source). We can prove a lemma `step_value_inv` or `SN_value : forall t, value t -> SN t`? Careful: value (abs) requires lc; but actually any term that isn't an app/if has no step. Let's prove `SN_abs : forall T t, SN (tm_abs T t)` and `SN_true`, `SN_false` similarly by inversion on step.

Second part: forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u).

Take u with LR T1 u. We need LR T2 (app (abs ...) u).

Now, using IH with a fresh x and sigma' := x |-> u ; sigma (mapping x to u, others to sigma). Need: for all y T, (x |-> T1 ; Gamma) y = Some T -> LR T (sigma' y). For y = x: sigma' x = u, needs LR T1 u ✓ (note (x|->T1;Gamma) x = Some T1 — needs Nat.eqb x x = true; yes with eq_refl-ish). For y ≠ x: Gamma y = Some T -> sigma' y = sigma y, which LR by hypothesis. Hmm, `update` uses `Nat.eqb x y`; need to handle.

Then IH gives: LR T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (tm_fvar x))) for any x not in L. We need to convert this into LR T2 (open (subst sigma t1) u) — actually we need LR T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u), so we use the step: app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u --> open (subst sigma t1) u (if value u and lc (abs)). And then to show LR T2 (app ... u), we need... hmm, SN of app requires the reduct open (subst sigma t1) u to be SN, and also for LR T2, if T2 is an arrow we need more: LR T2 (app ...u) = SN (app...) /\ forall v, LR T1... hmm.

Wait: LR T2 (app t u) — we want to derive this from LR T2 (open t1 u) where app t u --> open t1 u? Not directly: LR isn't preserved backwards by step in general. E.g., for arrow type T2 = A->B, LR T2 w requires SN w and for all v LR A v, LR B (app w v). If w --> w', we can use the standard "LR is closed under expansion by one step" lemma: if t --> t' and LR T t' then LR T t. This is provable by induction on T:
- Bool: SN t from SN t' and t-->t'.
- Arrow: need SN t (from SN t' plus step), and for all u LR T1 u, LR T2 (app t u). We have LR T2 (app t' u) by hypothesis (LR T2 t' includes that). And app t u --> app t' u (ST_App1, requires locally_closed u — ugh!). Then use IH T2: app t u --> app t' u, LR T2 (app t' u) gives LR T2 (app t u).

Here's where locally_closed appears! ST_App1 requires `locally_closed t2`. So the step `app t u --> app t' u` requires lc u. Hmm. So we need u locally closed. That means our LR should include locally_closed! Let's include it: LR T t := locally_closed t /\ (SN t) /\ (arrow stuff...). Then in the expansion lemma we have lc u from LR T1 u. That works.

Hmm, but does LR need lc in all cases? For the Bool case: LR Bool t := lc t /\ SN t. That's fine.

But then also the ST_AppAbs step requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T1 t1)`. In the T_Abs case, the term is `tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)` — is it locally closed? Hmm. Under a nonempty sigma containing non-closed terms, `subst sigma t1` is not locally closed (it's an open term). Hmm, actually locally_closed means no free bvars (fvar allowed). lc_at k allows tm_fvar freely. So `tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)` where sigma maps to arbitrary terms with fvars is locally closed as long as sigma's terms are locally closed AND t1 itself has no... wait subst sigma t1 replaces fvars with sigma terms, keeps bvars. If t1 has free bvars? t1 comes from a well-typed lambda, and well-typed terms are locally closed, so t1 has no free bvars (relative to 0). So subst sigma t1 has no free bvars provided sigma's images have no free bvars. So if LR includes lc, then u is lc. And sigma maps to LR terms which are lc. So abs is lc. 

Hmm, but wait: in the T_Abs case, we need `locally_closed (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1))` to apply ST_AppAbs. This means we need t1 (the body as given, with fvars representing bound vars) to be locally closed as a term — i.e., `lc_at 0 t1`, where fvars are considered local. Hmm, is that true from the typing derivation? In the typing rule, `open t1 (fvar x)` is typed, not t1 itself. But standard LN: body t1 is itself locally closed in the sense `lc_at 1 t1` (all bvars < appropriate depth). Hmm, if t1 has bvars referring to its own binder... Wait, in locally nameless, an abstraction `tm_abs T t1` has body t1 whose free bvar indices refer to enclosing binders. If we consider t1 in isolation, `lc_at 0 t1` would be false if it has a free bvar pointing to the lambda's own binder... no wait — t1's bvar 0 refers to the lambda's binder, so t1 as a standalone term has a free bvar 0, hence not `lc_at 0 t1`. But `lc_at 1 t1` holds if it's well-formed. Hmm, and `tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)` is `lc_at 0` iff `lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1)`.

OK so this gets into needing "typing implies locally closed" in the appropriate form: `has_type Gamma t T -> lc_at 0 t`? For a lambda `tm_abs T1 t1`, `has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) T` gives `lc_at 0 (tm_abs T1 t1)` which requires `lc_at 1 t1`. This is exactly the standard "typing → lc" lemma, provable from the fact that `open t1 (fvar x)` is typed and... hmm, actually to prove it, we would need: from `forall x fresh, has_type (x|->T1;Gamma) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`, deduce `lc_at 1 t1`. Standard LN lemmas: `lc_at` of open term implies lc of the term (a "closing" lemma). Ugh.

Alternative: include local-closure in a *different* way to sidestep. Hmm.

Hold on. Let's reconsider: maybe we don't need lc at all if we formulate the SN argument carefully. The issue arises when we need to derive step facts:

(a) app (abs T1 t1) u --> open t1 u requires lc (abs T1 t1) and value u.
(b) app t u --> app t' u requires step t t' and lc u.
(c) if t1 then t2 else t3 steps require lc t2, lc t3.
(d) if t1 t1' then ... requires lc t2, lc t3.

So the step relation only produces certain reducts when lc conditions hold. Note that these lc premises are *guards* — they restrict the relation. For SN (which is a "all reducts SN" property), having fewer reducts is easier. So we don't need lc to *apply* steps; we need lc to know that certain steps *exist* (e.g., to show that if u --> u' then app v u --> app v u' — we need lc? no, ST_App2 requires value v1, not lc of t2... wait ST_App2: value v1 -> t2 --> t2' -> app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2'. Yes no lc needed for t2 (its lc comes from... hmm, actually it doesn't require lc t2'). And ST_App1 requires lc t2.

Where do we need to *apply* steps? In the T_App case of the fundamental theorem: from LR (T1->T2) t1 and LR T1 t2, conclude LR T2 (app t1 t2) — that's given directly by the arrow LR definition (app t1 t2 ∈ LR T2). Good, no step needed.

For the T_Abs case: we need LR (T1->T2) (abs T1 t1). The arrow LR requires: SN (abs...), and for all u ∈ LR T1, LR T2 (app (abs) u). To show LR T2 (app (abs) u), we use the expansion lemma backwards along the step app (abs) u --> open t1 u, plus LR T2 (open t1 u) from the IH. Applying the expansion lemma requires the step to exist: requires lc (abs T1 t1) and value u. value u: LR T1 u must imply value u! Hmm, that's another standard property: LR T u -> value u for u in normal form? No — LR Bool true = SN true ∧ lc... doesn't imply value. Hmm. Wait: is it true that LR T u implies u is a value? For a term of type T that is SN, it could be a non-value stuck term like `fvar x` applied... e.g., LR Bool (fvar x) — SN (fvar x) holds (no steps) and lc holds. So not a value. So value u doesn't follow.

But in the step ST_AppAbs, we need `value v` where v is the argument — we can't get it. Hmm! Unless we add value to the LR! Standard CBV logical relations: for a *closed* term of arrow type, we want: either it's a value or it steps... The standard trick: define the LR for CBV as: 
- LR Bool t := SN t ∧ (t -->* true ∨ t -->* false)? Hmm.

Alternative standard trick: define LR with "SN and either value or ...". Actually the cleanest for CBV strong normalization: 

Define `LR T t` as:
- Bool: SN t
- Arrow T1 T2: SN t ∧ forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app t u)

and to handle the abs case, instead of using the step app (abs) u --> open t1 u, we *don't* need it if we prove: for all u ∈ LR T1, LR T2 (app (abs T1 t1) u). Expand: SN (app (abs) u) and for all v ∈ LR T1, LR T2 (app (app (abs) u) v)? Hmm, we need SN of app (abs) u. Its possible reducts: open t1 u (needs lc abs, value u), or app (abs) u' where u --> u' (needs value (abs), which needs lc abs... wait v_abs requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t); the argument v1 in ST_App2 is the abstraction, so we need value (abs T1 t1), needing lc).

Hmm, so to show SN (app (abs) u), we need to consider all reducts. Reducts of app (abs T1 t1) u:
- ST_AppAbs: open t1 u, requires lc (abs T1 t1) and value u.
- ST_App2: app (abs T1 t1) u' where u --> u' and value (abs T1 t1) — requires lc (abs T1 t1).

So to show SN, we need: (i) SN (open t1 u) [when applicable], (ii) for all u' with u --> u', SN (app (abs) u') [when value abs].

If we want to do this by induction, we might need strong induction on the SN derivation of u (u is SN since LR T1 u implies SN). And we need to handle the case where u is not a value: then ST_AppAbs doesn't apply, so only ST_App2 reducts, which still exist regardless of u being value. So SN (app (abs) u) for arbitrary SN u: standard "SN is preserved by application on the right when the left is a value"? Actually with the CBV rule requiring value v1 for ST_App2, we need value (abs) which requires lc abs. If lc abs holds, then app (abs) u is SN whenever u is SN? Hmm, not exactly: ST_AppAbs gives open t1 u — we need that SN too. And the standard approach: because open t1 u is SN whenever u is SN (by the IH from the LR substitution), and by induction on SN u, we get SN (app (abs) u).

But we can simplify: since we need value u for ST_AppAbs to fire, we can split: if u is a value, then the reduct open t1 u must be SN; if u is not a value, ST_AppAbs doesn't fire. In both cases, the ST_App2 reducts are app (abs) u' with u-->u'. So the natural approach: prove by induction on SN u (or on the multistep) that app (abs) u is SN given (forall u' with...?).

Actually, let's just define SN as the inductive accessibility and do well-founded induction. Standard: prove `SN_app_abs : forall T t u, locally_closed (tm_abs T t) -> SN u -> (forall u', value u' -> SN (open t u')) -> SN (app (tm_abs T t) u)`. Hmm, need "forall u' value, SN (open t u')" — but we only know it for u' in LR T1, not all values.

Hmm, but if u is SN and not a value, then reducts are only via ST_App2. If u is a value but not in LR... well, LR T1 u means SN u and lc u and (for arrow, more). But does LR T1 u imply value u when u is a "canonical" term? No, as noted (fvar x is SN, lc, but not a value of Bool). Hmm, but for the theorem we only care about *closed* terms, where fvar doesn't appear. But our LR-based IH involves open t1 (fvar x) etc. Hmm, those have fvars.

OK here's the thing: in the T_Abs case, the argument u in LR T1 u may be a stuck non-value (e.g., `fvar y`). Then ST_AppAbs cannot fire (needs value u), and we need only handle ST_App2 reducts: app (abs) u' with u --> u'. Since u ∈ LR T1 implies SN u, induction on that works — but we need to know app (abs) u' stays SN, i.e., an induction hypothesis matching. And if u is a value (and hence ST_AppAbs can fire), we need SN (open t1 u) — which we get from the IH via the substitution lemma, provided u ∈ LR T1.

So the correct lemma is:

`SN_app_abs : forall T t u, locally_closed (tm_abs T t) -> SN u -> (forall u', value u' -> LR T1 u' -> SN (open t u')) -> LR T1 u -> SN (app (tm_abs T t) u)`

Hmm, but the induction on SN u: SN u is `SN_intro u (forall u', u --> u' -> SN u')`. To prove SN (app (abs) u) by induction on SN u, we... SN is an inductive Prop, so we can do `induction HSN` and the IH would be about... hmm, we'd need the statement to be general enough over t. The SN derivation of u gives us for each reduct u', SN u' (as a Prop, not derivation). induction on SN u doesn't give IH for the reducts' derivations since SN u' appears as a premise in the constructor, and induction principle: `SN_ind : forall P, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> P t) -> forall t, SN t -> P t`? Let's check: the constructor is `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') -> SN t`. The induction principle P over `strongly_normalizing`:
`SN_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) -> forall t, SN t -> P t`. Yes! Because the recursive argument's IH applies to all t' with step t t' — and P t' is derived for each. Good, so induction on SN u with a suitably general statement (quantifying over t, T1... and maybe the extra hypotheses) works.

This is the standard "SN is closed under beta expansion" style proof.

Let me think about whether there's a simpler overall formulation that avoids all this pain. 

Alternative idea: Instead of using the given step relation's SN directly, define a *different*, more permissive reduction relation, prove SN w.r.t. it, and then transfer. E.g., define a "parallel reduction" or just a version of step without the `locally_closed` premises (call it `step'`). If `step ⊆ step'` then SN w.r.t. step' implies SN w.r.t. step. Actually we need: SN_{step} t follows from SN_{step'} t where step ⊆ step'. Since step ⊆ step' means more reducts in step', SN w.r.t. step' is stronger. So prove SN for a cleaner relation. But the LC premises are needed in the abs case anyway... Hmm, but if we drop the lc premises from step (call it step0 with no lc premises and value dropped for the app-abs rule), we get:

step0:
- app (abs T t) v --> open t v  (for ANY v, not just values)
- app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2
- app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2' (for any v1? no — need CBV: only when v1 is a value; if we allow any v1 then this becomes a non-deterministic relation where we also have the app-abs rule for non-abs terms...) 

Hmm, the problem: if we drop value conditions, the relation becomes non-deterministic in a way that may not be SN. E.g., `app (abs T t) u --> open t u` for any u and also `app (abs T t) u --> app (abs T t) u'` for any u --> u' — still SN if the body substitution gives SN for all SN u. Actually the SN for open t u requires u SN (that's what the LR substitution gives), which is fine for u ∈ LR.

I think the cleanest is: work with the given step, and handle lc by *including* lc in the LR and proving "typing → lc". Hmm, but "typing → lc" for LN with T_Abs rule (which types `open t1 (fvar x)`) requires a closing lemma: if `open t1 (fvar x)` is locally closed (for fresh x) then `t1` is lc_at 1. That's a standard LN lemma requiring reasoning about open_rec and fvars — doable but adds work (may need fvar freshness / permutation or use a specific fresh x via infinite atoms + "forall x not in L" gives us such x).

Alternatively: include in the LR only what's needed and avoid needing lc for the term `tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)`:

Do we actually need lc (abs) for anything? In the T_Abs case, we need to show LR (T1 -> T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) where the relevant step uses are:

Needed properties of LR:
(P1) LR T t → SN t. (by def)
(P2) LR T t → lc t. (by def if included)
(P3) Forward closure: LR T t → t --> t' → LR T t'. 
   - Bool: SN t' ✓.
   - Arrow: SN t' ✓; for u ∈ LR T1, need LR T2 (app t' u). From LR T2 (app t u) and step app t u --> app t' u (ST_App1, needs lc u — from P2 on u ✓). Use IH (P3 at T2) ✓.
(P4) Backward expansion: t --> t' → LR T t' → LR T t.
   - Bool: SN t from SN t', step ✓.
   - Arrow: SN t ✓; for u ∈ LR T1: LR T2 (app t' u) from LR T2 t' applied to u; then app t u --> app t' u (ST_App1 with lc u ✓); apply IH at T2 ✓.
(P5) For lambda: if for all u, LR T1 u → LR T2 (app (abs T1 t1) u) and SN(abs) then LR (T1->T2) (abs). Trivial by definition.

So we need (P2) lc in the LR, and we need lc to be preserved/produced. In the fundamental theorem, we'd prove `lc (subst sigma t) ∧ LR T (subst sigma t)`.

Now in the T_Abs case: goal `lc (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) ∧ LR (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1))`.
- lc: need lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1). From has_type we need the lc of the body... We know lc_at 0 (tm_abs T1 t1) = lc_at 1 t1 by typing→lc (on the *original* term, not substituted!). Then subst sigma t1: if sigma maps everything to lc terms, then subst of an lc term is lc. So we need: lc_at 1 t1 and (forall x, lc (sigma x)) → lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1). Wait, but does substituting fvars preserve lc_at levels? The bvar indices stay the same, fvars get replaced by sigma terms which must be lc at level 1 (i.e., no free bvars at all — lc_at 1 means bvars < 1, i.e., bvar 0 allowed! Hmm, lc_at 1 allows bvar 0 which is a *free* bvar when standalone). Hmm, careful: lc_at k t means all free bvar indices < k. For subst sigma t1 to be lc_at 1, we need sigma x to be lc_at 1 for all x (bvars < 1) and t1 lc_at 1. But actually sigma x terms are "locally closed" (lc_at 0), so they satisfy lc_at 1 too. Hmm wait, actually there's subtlety: `subst sigma t1` where t1 is lc_at 1 (has bvar 0 free, referring to the lambda binder) — after substitution, bvar 0 remains, so the result is lc_at 1 ✓ provided sigma images are lc_at 1. And since LR terms are lc_at 0 (from P2), and lc_at 0 → lc_at 1, ✓.

So the fundamental theorem's lc part requires the semantic context's images to be lc, which they are if LR terms are lc.

- LR (arrow) part: need SN (abs ...) ✓ trivial; and forall u, LR T1 u → LR T2 (app (abs ...) u).

Now the crux: for u ∈ LR T1, prove LR T2 (app (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u).

We know (from IH, with sigma' = update sigma x u and fresh x):
  LR T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))).
And the equation subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u (needs x fresh w.r.t. t1 and sigma' = x|->u;sigma).

```
strongly_normalizing_ind :
 forall P : tm -> Prop,
 (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
 forall t, SN t -> P t
```
We can use `induction HSN_u` and get IH: forall t', u --> t' -> P t'. But note the induction principle requires P to be a fixed predicate of the term; we can make P depend on parameters (T2, t', ...) by generalizing before induction: `remember`/revert.

Actually here's a cleaner idea used in many developments: define the logical relation via a "closure" that includes multi-step reduction:

`LR T t := forall t', t -->* t' -> ...` — i.e., define LR such that it's closed under reduction by construction. E.g.:

- `LR Bool t := SN t`
- `LR (A->B) t := SN t /\ forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app t u)`

**KEY IDEA**: Handle it by proving a lemma that `LR T` is closed under backward expansion *for any number of steps*, i.e., define `LR` in terms of a value-based semantics:

`LR T t := SN t ∧ ∀ t', t -->* t' → (value t' → VR T t')` where `VR` is on values:
- `VR Bool v := True` (v true or false, but any Bool-typed value is true/false)
- `VR (A->B) v := ∀ u, LR A u → LR B (app v u)`.

Now, prove:
(i) `LR T t` for a stuck/neutral term t (no steps, not a value): SN ✓, and no value-reducts except t itself which is not a value → vacuous ✓. So any stuck term is in LR at any type — WITHOUT needing lc! 
(ii) For an abstraction `abs T t'`: SN ✓; value-reducts: only itself (values don't step), so need `VR (A->B) (abs T1 t')`, i.e., ∀u ∈ LR A, LR B (app (abs) u). So the VR condition for app (abs) u is: for all value-reducts w of app (abs) u, VR B w — vacuous! And then LR B (app (abs) u) = SN ∧ all value-reducts VR — which is exactly what we just showed!! 

So with this "value-based" LR, the arrow case's nested application is handled because `LR B (app (abs) u)` for B = C->D only requires SN and value-reduct conditions, and the value-reducts are vacuous for applications. Great, the value-based LR seems very promising! Let me now define it carefully.

```
Definition SN := strongly_normalizing.

Fixpoint VR (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app v u)
  end
with LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  SN t /\ forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> VR T t'.
```

Hmm, mutual fixpoint between VR and LR on the type. The type argument decreases in both. In VR's arrow case, we use LR T1 and LR T2 where T1, T2 are subterms ✓. In LR's definition, VR T uses the same T but LR calls VR... The mutual fixpoint: LR T depends on VR T (same T) and VR (T1->T2) depends on LR T1, LR T2 (smaller). Coq's guard checker: a mutual fixpoint where LR calls VR on the same argument and VR calls LR on smaller arguments — is that accepted? The guard condition for mutual fixpoints... I think it should be fine if we structure it as a single Fixpoint returning a pair, or use two mutual fixpoints where recursion is on the type. Actually, "LR T" calling "VR T" is not a recursive call on a smaller argument (same T), which might be rejected by the guard checker for mutual definitions, since in a mutual block, calls must be on structurally smaller arguments. Hmm. But we can inline VR into LR:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t /\ forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t' u))
  end.
```
With `True` for the Bool VR. Simplify: `LR Bool t := SN t` and `LR (T1->T2) t := SN t /\ (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app t' u))`.

This is a single Fixpoint on T with recursive calls on T1 and T2 ✓. Note the "SN t" is at each type. Nice.

Actually, can we simplify further: `LR (T1->T2) t := SN t ∧ ∀ t' , t -->* t' → value t' → ∀ u, LR T1 u → LR T2 (app t' u)`. Hmm, since values don't step, "value-reducts of t'" — including t' itself when t' is a value (multi_refl) ✓.

Now the properties:
(1) LR T t → SN t. ✓ (first component; by destruct T; both cases have SN first).
(2) Forward closure: LR T t → t --> t' → LR T t'. 
    - Bool: SN t' from SN t and step ✓ (needs lemma SN_step: SN t → t --> t' → SN t').

(3) Backward expansion: LR T t' → t --> t' → LR T t.
    - Bool: SN t from SN t' + step (need lemma SN_step_back: SN t' → t --> t' → SN t).
    - Arrow: SN t ✓; for w with t -->* w, value w: if t -->* w via multi_refl then w = t and value t — but t --> t' contradicts value t (values don't step)... Hmm, we need: t -->* w with value w. Otherwise multi_step t t1 ... where t --> t1; hmm t1 could be t' or... the derivation is t --> t1 -->* w. We need to show ∀u ∈ LR T1, LR T2 (app w u). We know from LR T t' (and forward closure? no) — hmm, we know t' -->* ... hmm, is t' -->* w? Not necessarily: t --> t1 and t1 -->* w where t1 = t' (since the only step from t... no! there could be multiple steps from t if t is not deterministic). Oh wait, t --> t' and t -->* w means the first step of that multistep might be some other t1. Hmm. So the backward expansion needs determinism?! 

Hmm! Careful. In CBV STLC the step relation is deterministic (for lc terms), but proving backward expansion of LR requires: any value-reduct w of t is also a value-reduct of t'. That requires determinism: t --> t', t --> t1 ⟹ t1 = t'. Let me re-plan the whole proof with the value-based LR:

Fundamental theorem: has_type Gamma t T → sem_ctx Gamma sigma → LR T (subst sigma t).

- T_Var: Gamma x = Some T → sem_ctx gives LR T (sigma x) = LR T (subst sigma (fvar x)) ✓.
- T_True/T_False: LR Bool true = SN true ✓ (value, no steps).
- T_App: have LR (T1->T2) (subst sigma t1) and LR T1 (subst sigma t2). Need LR T2 (app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)). Here we need the application property of LR at arrow type. This is the key lemma discussed. Needs:
   `LR_app : forall A B t u, LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u)`.
- T_If: have LR Bool (subst sigma t1) (so SN), LR T (subst sigma t2), LR T (subst sigma t3). Need LR T (if t1 then t2 else t3). Reducts: if t1 --> t1' → if t1' t2 t3; if t1 is true/false → t2/t3 (needs lc's!). Hmm: SN of the if-term: reducts are (a) t2 (requires lc t2 — hmm, ST_IfTrue requires lc t1 and lc t2 — wait, ST_IfTrue: locally_closed t1 -> locally_closed t2 -> if true t1 t2 --> t1). So if lc fails, fewer reducts. And (b) via ST_If from t1's reducts.
  We know LR T t2 (full LR, so SN ✓) and LR T t3, and LR Bool t1 (SN). Need SN (if t1 t2 t3) and value-reducts condition. Reducts: 
   * ST_IfTrue: reduct t2 → need LR T t2 (have it ✓, but wait we need "SN of reduct" and "value-reduct condition" — LR T t2 is exactly what we need for forward closure... hmm, we need SN (reduct) and the VR condition; LR T t2 gives both ✓).
   * ST_IfFalse: similarly t3 ✓.
   * ST_If: reduct if t1' t2 t3 with t1 --> t1' → need SN (if t1' t2 t3). By induction on SN t1! Since t1' is a reduct, we'd need the statement for t1'. So we need an induction on SN t1 proving SN (if t1 t2 t3) given SN t2, SN t3.
   Then for the value-reduct condition of LR T (if ...): any value-reduct w of the if-term. Hmm, e.g., if t1 -->* true then the if-term -->* t2 -->* w. So value-reducts include value-reducts of t2 (and t3, and reducts through t1). We need: for all w with (if t1 t2 t3) -->* w and value w, VR T w. Hmm, we know LR T t2 = SN t2 ∧ ∀ w, t2 -->* w → value w → VR T w. So value-reducts of t2 are OK. But we need to relate "w reachable from the if-term" to "w reachable from t2/t3". That requires analyzing the multistep derivation: any multistep from `if t1 t2 t3` either stays as an if-term (reducing t1) until t1 becomes true/false, then continues into t2/t3, or gets stuck. Formalizing this requires induction on the multistep derivation and case analysis on the step, threading an invariant.

This is getting heavy but is doable. However, maybe there's a simpler LR formulation that avoids the "value-reduct" complexity, e.g., the "SN plus application" formulation, but we struggled with the abs case there. Let's compare.

Alternative LR formulation (call it LR2):
- LR2 Bool t := SN t
- LR2 (A->B) t := SN t ∧ ∀u, LR2 A u → LR2 B (app t u).

The abs case problem: LR2 (A->B) (abs T1 t') requires ∀u ∈ LR2 A, LR2 B (app (abs) u). The value-based LR (LR) handles this by noting that LR B (app (abs) u) follows from SN + vacuous value-reducts... but wait, with LR (value-based), the goal LR B (app (abs) u) requires the value-reduct condition: for all value-reducts w of app (abs) u, VR B w. As I argued, no reduct of app (abs) u is a value (since app(...) is never a value, and all reducts of app(...) are apps or opened terms — hmm, opened terms could be values! e.g., open t' u could be `true` if t' = bvar 0, but that's not from a well-typed abs... hmm, t' = subst sigma t1; could open t' u = true? If t1 = bvar 0 then open t1 u = u, so subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = subst sigma' (fvar x) = u and open (subst sigma t1) u = open (bvar 0) u = u. So the reduct is u, which IS a value if u is a value!). So the value-reducts of app (abs) u can include values obtained after beta. So we DO need: value-reducts of app (abs) u satisfy VR B. 

Hmm, in the value-based LR, LR B (open t' u) is known from Hsub ✓, and if the beta happens then the reduct open t' u ∈ LR B ✓, so its value-reducts satisfy VR B ✓ (from LR B's definition: all value-reducts of open t' u satisfy VR B, and value-reducts of app (abs) u = value-reducts of open t' u when beta fires). I think there might be a slicker path. Let's think about using a **"reduction-closed" LR with values**:

Hmm, but the natural way: define LR by recursion on the SN derivation! Since SN is inductive, we can define LR by recursion on the SN derivation of t:

`LR T t` for an SN term t: by SN_intro, t has reducts t' each SN. Define LR T t := (value t → VR T t) ∧ ∀ t', t --> t' → LR T t'. This is well-defined by recursion on the SN derivation. Since SN derivations are unique? Hmm, but the definition should be independent of the derivation. Practically, we can define LR as an inductive predicate that mirrors this:

```
Inductive LR (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
| LR_intro : forall t, SN t -> (value t -> VR T t) -> (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t
```
with VR defined mutually... but VR references LR at smaller types, and LR's constructor references VR at the same type. Structurally, LR T's constructor mentions VR T (same T) — but VR doesn't need to be mutually defined with LR if we inline: 

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ (value t -> forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app t u)) end.
```
Hmm, this definition (with SN included, value-condition) is an inductive-recursive-looking definition but actually it's just a Fixpoint on T since LR T1, LR T2 are subterms. But then the "∀ t', t --> t' → LR T t'" closure isn't part of the definition. Hmm.

```coq
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      SN t /\
      (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' ->
         forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t' u))
  end.
```

Now let's also handle the T_Abs case (proving LR (A->B) (abs T1 t')):

- SN ✓.
- The multistep from abs to t'': since abs doesn't step, t'' = abs (multi_refl). So need ∀ u ∈ LR A, LR B (app (abs T1 t') u). This is the "app abs" lemma:

`LR_app_abs : forall A B (t' : tm) (u : tm), lc (tm_abs T1 t') -> (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR B (open t' u)) -> LR T1 u -> LR B (app (tm_abs T1 t') u)`.

  * If u is a value: first step is beta → z = open t' u; then w is a value-reduct of open t' u; since LR B (open t' u) (from Hsub) ✓ → VR B w ✓.

Great — so `LR_app_abs` is provable by induction on SN u. And `LR_app` by induction on SN t. Both need the "first-step decomposition" of multistep and the stuck-term lemma.

Let me now define `subst`:

```
Fixpoint subst (s : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => s x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst s t1) (subst s t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst s t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst s t1) (subst s t2) (subst s t3)
  end.
```

Lemma: `subst_open`: for x, u, s, t with x not free in t... Let's define `fv_occurs x t` (x occurs free in t) or use a predicate. We need: `subst (update_s s x u) (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst s t) u`, provided x does not occur free in t.

```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom := ...
Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L).
Lemma fresh_spec : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Lemma fresh_fv : forall L t, ~ In (fresh (L ++ fv t)) (fv t) etc.
```

Definition upd (s : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=

So: final proof: `apply fundamental with (sigma := fun x => tm_fvar x)`; sem_ctx empty id: intros x T H; rewrite empty definition in H: `empty x = None` — need to show `None = Some T` is impossible → discriminate ✓. Then LR T t → SN t ✓ (first component).

Let me define things concretely:

```coq
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' ->
         forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t' u))
  end.
```

Hmm, one concern: `LR` is a Fixpoint returning Prop with recursive calls on T1, T2 ✓ structurally decreasing.

Wait, but hmm: `LR T1 u` where u : tm — fine.

Potential issue: Coq's guard checker with a Fixpoint returning Prop and recursive calls in the arrow case — standard, fine.

Now the lemmas:

**L1: SN_step**: `forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'`.
Proof: `intros t t' H; induction H as [t Hred]; intros Hstep; apply Hred; exact Hstep.` Hmm, `induction H` where H : SN t — pattern: `intros t t' HSN; remember ... ` Actually simplest: `intros t t' HSN; induction HSN as [t Hred IH]; intros Hstep; apply Hred; exact Hstep.` Hmm, `induction HSN` generalizes t; the goal must mention t. Let's do: `intros t t' HSN Hstep. induction HSN as [t Hred]. apply Hred. exact Hstep.` Should work if the goal is `SN t'` and Hstep : step t t' — the case has Hred : forall t', step t t' -> SN t'; apply Hred; exact Hstep ✓. But careful: `induction HSN` will try to generalize dependent things; since t' and Hstep don't mention... Hstep mentions t. Need `generalize dependent t'`? Hstep : step t t' involves t. Coq's `induction HSN` will revert t' and Hstep? No — `induction` on a hypothesis in the context generalizes the *goal* over the indices... Actually, for `HSN : strongly_normalizing t`, `induction HSN` gives cases with t fixed? Let's think: the induction principle SN_ind : forall P, (...) -> forall t, SN t -> P t. `induction HSN` where the goal is P t' (dependent on t'? no, on t and t'). Hmm, the goal is `strongly_normalizing t'` which doesn't mention t, but Hstep does. Coq will complain "t' and Hstep depend on t" unless we revert them. - `SN_step : SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'`.

**L2: LR_SN**: `forall T t, LR T t -> SN t`. By destruct T; simpl; tauto/intuition.

**L3: LR_forward**: `forall T t t', LR T t -> step t t' -> LR T t'`.
Proof: destruct T; simpl in *.
- Bool: apply SN_step ✓.
- Arrow: destruct HLR as [HSN HVR]. Split.
  * SN_step ✓.
  * intros w Hmulti Hval u HLRu. Apply HVR with w; [|exact Hval]; apply multi_step with t'; [exact Hstep | exact Hmulti] ✓. Wait: multi step t' w from step t t' and multi t' w: `multi_step t t' w Hstep Hmulti` ✓.

**L4: LR_stuck / LR_no_step**: if t has no steps and is not a value, then LR T t. Let's state: `forall T t, (forall t', ~ (t --> t')) -> ~ value t -> LR T t`. 
Proof: destruct T; simpl.
- Bool: SN_intro; intros t' H; exfalso; exact (H t' Hstep).
- Arrow: split.
  * SN_intro; intros; exfalso; apply (H t' Hstep) ✓.
  * intros w Hmulti Hval u HLRu. exfalso. Case on multistep: by induction — hmm, need: no multistep from t to a value if t has no steps and isn't a value. Prove `multi_no_step : forall t w, (forall z, ~ (t --> z)) -> multi step t w -> w = t`. Hmm, that's a general lemma: if t has no steps then t -->* w implies w = t. Proof by induction on the multistep derivation: base ✓; step: t --> z contradicts ✓. But the general statement should be for the *first* term; when we get `multi_step t z w Hstep _`, Hstep : t --> z contradicts the no-step hypothesis ✓.
    So: `assert (w = t)` by (induction Hmulti; auto; exfalso; apply (H z); assumption) then subst, and apply Hval → contradiction with Hnotval ✓.

Let me define a general lemma: `multi_of_no_step : forall t w, (forall z, ~ step t z) -> multi step t w -> w = t`.

**L5: multi_first**: `forall t w, multi step t w -> w = t \/ (exists z, step t z /\ multi step z w)`.
Proof by induction on the multistep derivation:
- multi_refl: left ✓.
- multi_step t z w Hstep Hmulti: right; exists z; split; [exact Hstep | exact Hmulti] ✓.

**L6: abs_no_step**: `forall T t z, ~ (tm_abs T t --> z)`. By intro; inversion.

And the value-reducts part: multi_first gives either w = app t u (impossible since value) or a step (contradiction) ✓. So the stuck case is handled *automatically* by the general proof! Because we never case-split on whether t is a value — we only analyze the steps, and if there are none, the proofs are vacuous ✓✓.

So the LR_app proof structure:
```
Lemma LR_app : forall A B t u, LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u).
Proof.
  intros A B t u HLRt HLRu.
  pose proof (LR_SN _ _ HLRt) as HSNt.
  pose proof (LR_SN _ _ HLRu) as HSNu.
  revert A B HLRt HLRu HSNu.   (* generalize over the argument side *)
  induction HSNt as [t Hredt IHt].
  intros A B HLRt HLRu HSNu.
  revert A HLRt HLRu HSNu? 
```

Then inside each case, we do inner induction on HSNu (SN u) with statement Q(u) := forall A B t, LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u) — but t is fixed in the outer case, so Q(u) := forall A B, LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u). Hmm, but the outer IH needs to be used with different u — fine, it's universally quantified over u ✓.

The inner induction on SN u: IH_u : forall u', step u u' -> Q(u') ✓.

Let me now think about the fundamental theorem in full:

```
Definition sem_ctx (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).

Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty; intros sigma Hctx; simpl.
  - (* T_Var *) apply Hctx. exact H.
  - (* T_Abs *) ...
  - (* T_App *) apply LR_app with (A := T1); [exact IHHty1 ... | exact IHHty2 ...].
  ...
```
Careful: the IH for T_Abs is `forall sigma, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> LR (T1->T2) (subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1))`? Hmm, the IH from `induction Hty` will be about the premise `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...`. The premise is a universally quantified statement, so the induction hypothesis would be for each x... Hmm, the induction principle for has_type: T_Abs's premise is `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` — a forall type, so there's NO induction hypothesis generated for it by Coq's default scheme, because the recursive occurrence is under a binder/forall. Actually, Coq's induction principle generation: for T_Abs, the argument is `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...` — the recursive occurrence appears in the codomain of a forall, which Coq does handle? Coq's default scheme generation for inductive predicates: it generates IH only for recursive occurrences in the conclusion or in the premises in a "positive" position at the top level. For premises of the form `forall x, P x -> has_type ...`, I believe Coq's scheme will NOT generate an IH (it only handles direct premises and foralls?). Let me recall: for an inductive like

```
Inductive has_type : ... :=
| T_Abs : forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2)
```
the generated induction principle `has_type_ind` will have the T_Abs case as:
```
forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) -> (forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) -> P Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2)
```
Yes! Coq does generate the IH under the forall (it handles products and foralls in the premise). I'm fairly confident: Coq's scheme generation handles premises that are products, generating IH for recursive occurrences in the conclusion of the product. Yes, standard (like the `T_Abs` in SF's Stlc — indeed in SF's STLC_Prop, `induction Hty` for T_Abs gives an IH `forall x, x \notin L -> P ...`).

So `induction Hty` gives IH for T_Abs as: `forall x, x \notin L -> (forall sigma, sem_ctx (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> LR T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x))))` ✓.

Great. Now the T_Abs case:
Goal: `LR (T1->T2) (subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1))` = LR (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) (by simpl).
Unfold LR (T1->T2): SN (abs) ∧ ∀ t'', multi step (abs) t'' → value t'' → ∀ u, LR T1 u → LR T2 (app t'' u).
- SN: SN_abs.
- intros t'' Hmulti Hval u HLRu. From multi_first: t'' = abs T1 (subst sigma t1) (no step) → subst. Then need LR T2 (app (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u).
  Apply LR_app_abs with Hsub := (fun w HLRw => ...). For each w ∈ LR T1, we need LR T2 (open (subst sigma t1) w):
    pick x fresh (x ∉ L, x ∉ fv t1); apply IH x Hx_absurd with sigma' := upd sigma x w:
      * sem_ctx (update Gamma x T1) (upd sigma x w): intros y Ty Hy. 
        destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E. 
        + (* y = x *) Nat.eqb_eq → subst y. Now Hy : update Gamma x T1 x = Some Ty. update Gamma x T1 x = Some T1, so Ty = T1. Need LR T1 (upd sigma x w x) = LR T1 w ✓.
          Prove `update Gamma x T1 x = Some T1` by unfold update; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl; auto. So Hy : Some T1 = Some Ty → injection → Ty = T1 → subst ✓.
        + (* y ≠ x *) Hy : update Gamma x T1 y = Some Ty → since eqb x y = false, update ... y = Gamma y → Hctx y Ty Hy ✓. And (upd sigma x w) y = sigma y (eqb false) ✓.
      * Gives LR T2 (subst (upd sigma x w) (open t1 (fvar x))).
      * Rewrite subst_open_fvar : subst (upd sigma x w) (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) w ✓ (needs x ∉ fv t1).
  So Hsub : forall w, LR T1 w -> LR T2 (open (subst sigma t1) w) ✓.
  Then LR_app_abs Hsub HLRu ✓ — note it gives LR T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) u) ✓ matching the goal after subst t''.

- T_App case: goal LR T2 (subst sigma (tm_app t1 t2)) = LR T2 (tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)). Apply LR_app with (A := T1) ✓ IHHty1, IHHty2 ✓.
- T_True: LR Bool tm_true = SN tm_true ✓.
- T_False ✓.
- T_If: LR T (tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)). Apply LR_if; IHHty1 gives LR Bool (subst sigma t1) = SN (subst sigma t1) ✓ (since LR Bool t = SN t definitionally); IHHty2, IHHty3 ✓.

Great. Then the final theorem:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T, empty |-- t \in T -> SN t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  pose proof (fundamental Gamma:=empty t T Hty (fun x => tm_fvar x)) as HLR.
  + intros x Ty Hx; unfold empty in Hx; discriminate.
  + apply LR_SN in HLR. rewrite subst_id in HLR. exact HLR.
Qed.
```
Where `subst_id : forall t, subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t` ✓ by induction.

Now, `multi_first`:
```
Lemma multi_first : forall (t w : tm), multi step t w -> w = t \/ (exists z, step t z /\ multi step z w).
Proof. intros t w H. induction H as [x | x y z Hstep Hmulti IH]. - left; reflexivity. - right; exists y; split; assumption. Qed.
```
Hmm: `induction H` on `multi step t w`: the constructors: multi_refl x : multi R x x; multi_step x y z : R x y -> multi R y z -> multi R x z. The induction principle: for P : forall t w, multi step t w -> Prop... Coq's `induction H` gives:
- case multi_refl: goal `forall... ` hmm, the goal mentions t and w which are instantiated. `induction H` where H : multi step t w and goal `w = t \/ ...` — Coq will generalize t and w appropriately (since they're indices, Coq's induction on an indexed inductive generalizes the indices in the goal ✓). So:
  - multi_refl x: goal `x = x \/ ...` → left; reflexivity ✓.
  - multi_step x y z Hxy Hyz: goal `z = x \/ exists z0, step x z0 /\ multi step z0 z`; IH : `z = y \/ (exists z0, step y z0 /\ multi step z0 z)`? Hmm, Coq's induction principle for `multi`:
    ```
    multi_ind : forall (X) (R) (P : forall x y, multi R x y -> Prop),
      (forall x, P x x (multi_refl R x)) ->
      (forall x y z (Hxy : R x y) (Hyz : multi R y z), P y z Hyz -> P x z (multi_step R x y z Hxy Hyz)) ->
      forall x y (H : multi R x y), P x y H
    ```
    So the IH is `P y z Hyz` — but our goal is about t and w which are generalized? If the goal is `w = t \/ ...` with t, w free variables and H : multi step t w, `induction H` will revert t and w? Actually, Coq's `induction` on an indexed hypothesis requires generalizing the indices; it does it automatically if they're variables. The resulting cases would be: for the multi_refl case, the goal becomes `x = x \/ ...` (with t := x, w := x) ✓; and for the multi_step case, goal `z = x \/ ...` with IH about y, z ✓. But the IH is P y z Hyz = `z = y \/ exists z0, step y z0 /\ multi step z0 z` ✓ — which we don't actually need. We just need `right; exists y; split; [exact Hxy | exact Hyz]` ✓.

OK good.

Now, LR_if's proof: similar.
```
Lemma LR_if : forall T t1 t2 t3, SN t1 -> LR T t2 -> LR T t3 -> LR T (tm_if t1 t2 t3).
```
Proof: revert T t2 t3; induction on SN t1; intros T t2 t3 HLR2 HLR3. Destruct T.
- Bool: SN_intro; intros r Hstep; step_if_inv:
  * ST_IfTrue: r = t2 → SN t2 from LR_SN ✓.
  * ST_IfFalse: r = t3 ✓.
  * ST_If: r = if t1' t2 t3, t1 --> t1' → IH t1' Hstep T t2 t3 HLR2 HLR3 : LR Bool (if ...) → SN ✓.
- Arrow C->D: split.
  * SN: same as above but with LR (C->D) and IH giving LR (C->D) ✓.
  * value-reducts: intros w Hmulti Hval v HLRv. multi_first; case:
    - w = if t1 t2 t3 → value_if_inv contradiction ✓.
    - step_if_inv:
      + ST_IfTrue: z = t2 → LR (C->D) t2's value-reduct condition with Hmulti' ✓, Hval, HLRv ✓.
      + ST_IfFalse: z = t3 ✓.
      + ST_If: z = if t1' t2 t3 → IH at t1' gives LR (C->D) z; its value-reduct condition ✓.
  
✓ Same shape.

Now, the induction for LR_if: `induction HSN1` where the goal is `forall T t2 t3, LR T t2 -> LR T t3 -> LR T (if t1 t2 t3)` — P(t1) ✓; the IH for t1' with step t1 t1' ✓.

Now let's also double check: in the ST_If case, the step_if_inv gives `t1 --> t1'` and lc's; we use IH ✓ which requires SN t1' — from Hred ✓ (the SN constructor's recursive premise).

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with

  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2

  end.
```

Lemma lc_at_open_fvar_inv : forall (j : nat) (x : atom) (t : tm),
  lc_at j (open_rec j (tm_fvar x) t) -> lc_at (S j) t.

    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. inversion H as [k i' Hi' | | | | | | ]; subst.  (* lc_bvar case *) 
      apply lc_bvar. lia.  (* i < j from H : lc_at j (bvar i) *)

Now the `fresh` lemma:
```
Fixpoint fresh (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | a :: L' => Nat.max (S a) (fresh L')
  end.
Lemma fresh_not_In : forall L x, In x L -> x < fresh L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L' IH]; intros x Hx.
  - inversion Hx.
  - simpl in *. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.lt_le_trans with (S a). apply Nat.lt_succ_diag_r. apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.lt_le_trans with (fresh L'). apply IH; assumption. apply Nat.le_max_r.
Qed.
Lemma fresh_spec : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof. intros L Hx. pose proof (fresh_not_In L (fresh L) Hx). lia. Qed.
```

  - ST_App1: t = app t1 t2, t' = app t1' t2, premise H : t1 --> t1'; goal lc_at 0 (app t1' t2); inversion Hlc gives lc t1, lc t2; apply lc_app; [apply IHHstep | ]; assumption.

Qed.
```
Order of arguments for lc_at_open: `lc_at_open : forall (t u : tm), lc_at 1 t -> lc_at 0 u -> lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 u t)` ✓.

Now let's write the LR lemmas.

`LR_SN : forall T t, LR T t -> strongly_normalizing t`:
```
intros T t. destruct T; simpl; intros [_ H]; exact H.
```
For Bool: LR = lc /\ SN → second ✓. For Arrow: lc /\ SN /\ cond → second ✓. So `destruct T; simpl; intros H; tauto` or `intuition`.

`LR_lc : forall T t, LR T t -> lc_at 0 t`: first component ✓.

`LR_forward : forall T t t', LR T t -> step t t' -> LR T t'`.
```
intros T t t' HLR Hstep. destruct T as [| A B]; simpl in *.
- destruct HLR as [Hlc Hsn]. split. apply lc_step with t; assumption. apply SN_step with t; assumption.
- destruct HLR as [Hlc [Hsn Hvr]]. split; [| split].
  + apply lc_step with t; assumption.
  + apply SN_step with t; assumption.
  + intros w Hmulti Hval u HLRu. apply Hvr with w; auto. apply multi_step with t'; auto.
    Hmm: Hvr : forall t', multi step t t' -> value t' -> forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app t' u). We need LR B (app w u) where multi step t' w. Apply Hvr with (t' := w): need multi step t w ← multi_step t t' w Hstep Hmulti ✓, value w ✓, u ✓.
Qed.
```

`SN_step`:
```
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.

```

Let's see: `intros t t' Hsn. revert t'. induction Hsn.` — the goal is `forall t', step t t' -> SN t'` which mentions t ✓. `induction Hsn` generalizes t ✓ giving:
- case: for t with Hred : forall t', step t t' -> SN t', IH : forall t', step t t' -> (forall t'', step t' t'' -> SN t'')? Hmm no. Let me think about the induction principle:

`strongly_normalizing_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) -> forall t, SN t -> P t.`

With P(t) := forall t', step t t' -> SN t'. Then the step case requires: forall t, Hred : forall t', step t t' -> SN t', IH : forall t', step t t' -> P t' ⊢ P t = forall t', step t t' -> SN t'. So `intros t' Hstep; apply Hred; exact Hstep` ✓ — the IH isn't even needed ✓.

In Coq: `intros t t' Hsn. revert t'. induction Hsn as [t Hred IH]. intros t' Hstep. apply Hred. exact Hstep.` Hmm, after `revert t'`, the goal is `forall t', step t t' -> SN t'` ✓ and Hsn : SN t. `induction Hsn` should work ✓.

(** * Auxiliary definitions and lemmas for strong normalization. *)

(* ---------- basic facts about strongly_normalizing ---------- *)

Lemma strong_norm_step : forall t t',
  strongly_normalizing t -> t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' Hsn. revert t'.
  induction Hsn as [t Hred IH].
  intros t' Hstep. apply Hred. exact Hstep.
Qed.
```

(** ** Basic inversion principles for the step relation *)

Let me write the file.

```coq
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => lc_at 0 t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      lc_at 0 t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall (t' : tm), multi step t t' -> value t' ->
        forall (u : tm), LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t' u))
  end.
```

Now `LR_SN`:
```coq
Lemma LR_SN : forall (T : ty) (t : tm), LR T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros T t. destruct T as [| T1 T2]; simpl; intros [_ H]; exact H.
Qed.
```
For arrow: LR = lc /\ SN /\ cond — pattern [_ H] with H : SN ∧ cond? Hmm: the conjunction is right-associative: lc /\ (SN /\ cond). So `intros [_ [H _]]` for arrow and `[_ H]` for Bool. Let me write:
```coq
Proof.
  intros T t. destruct T as [| T1 T2]; simpl; intros H.
  - destruct H as [_ H]. exact H.
  - destruct H as [_ [H _]]. exact H.
Qed.
```

`LR_lc`:
```coq
Lemma LR_lc : forall (T : ty) (t : tm), LR T t -> lc_at 0 t.
Proof.
  intros T t. destruct T as [| T1 T2]; simpl; intros H.
  - destruct H as [H _]. exact H.
  - destruct H as [H _]. exact H.
Qed.
```

`LR_forward`:
```coq

```coq
(** * Auxiliary syntactic infrastructure *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.

Fixpoint fresh (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | a :: L' => Nat.max (S a) (fresh L')
  end.

Lemma fresh_not_In : forall (L : list atom) (x : atom), In x L -> x < fresh L.
Proof.
  intros L. induction L as [| a L' IH]; intros x Hx.
  - inversion Hx.
  - simpl in *. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.lt_le_trans with (S a). apply Nat.lt_succ_diag_r. apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.lt_le_trans with (fresh L'). apply IH; assumption. apply Nat.le_max_r.
Qed.
```
Hmm, `simpl in *` might simplify `fresh (a :: L')` to `Nat.max (S a) (fresh L')` ✓.

```coq
Lemma fresh_spec : forall (L : list atom), ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hx. pose proof (fresh_not_In L (fresh L) Hx). lia.
Qed.
```

Now `subst`:
```coq
Definition upd (s : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else s y.

Fixpoint subst (s : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => s x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst s t1) (subst s t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst s t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst s t1) (subst s t2) (subst s t3)
  end.
```

```coq
Lemma step_app_inv : forall (t1 t2 r : tm),
  tm_app t1 t2 --> r ->
  (exists (T0 : ty) (t0 : tm),
      t1 = tm_abs T0 t0 /\ locally_closed (tm_abs T0 t0) /\ value t2 /\ r = open t0 t2)
  \/ (exists t1', t1 --> t1' /\ locally_closed t2 /\ r = tm_app t1' t2)
  \/ (exists (v1 : tm) (t2' : tm),
      t1 = v1 /\ value v1 /\ t2 --> t2' /\ r = tm_app v1 t2').
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 r H. inversion H; subst.
  - left. eauto.
  - right. left. eauto.
  - right. right. eauto.
Qed.
```

Lemma lc_step : forall (t t' : tm), t --> t' -> lc_at 0 t -> lc_at 0 t'.

```

```coq
(** * Infrastructure for the strong normalization proof *)

(** ** Free variables and freshness *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.

Fixpoint fresh (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | a :: L' => Nat.max (S a) (fresh L')
  end.

Lemma fresh_not_In : forall (L : list atom) (x : atom), In x L -> x < fresh L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L' IH]; intros x Hx.
  - inversion Hx.
  - simpl. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.lt_le_trans with (S a).
      * apply Nat.lt_succ_diag_r.
      * apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.lt_le_trans with (fresh L').
      * apply IH; assumption.
      * apply Nat.le_max_r.
Qed.

Lemma fresh_spec : forall (L : list atom), ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hx. pose proof (fresh_not_In L (fresh L) Hx). lia.
Qed.

Lemma fresh_not_In_app_l : forall (L M : list atom), ~ In (fresh (L ++ M)) L.
Proof.
  intros L M H. apply (fresh_spec (L ++ M)). apply in_or_app. left. exact H.
Qed.

Lemma fresh_not_In_app_r : forall (L M : list atom), ~ In (fresh (L ++ M)) M.
Proof.
  intros L M H. apply (fresh_spec (L ++ M)). apply in_or_app. right. exact H.
Qed.

(** ** Free-variable substitution *)

Definition upd (s : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else s y.

Fixpoint subst (s : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => s x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst s t1) (subst s t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst s t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst s t1) (subst s t2) (subst s t3)
  end.

Lemma subst_id : forall (t : tm), subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof.
  induction t; simpl; try reflexivity; try congruence.
Qed.

Lemma upd_eq : forall (s : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm), upd s x u x = u.
Proof. intros. unfold upd. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma upd_neq : forall (s : atom -> tm) (x y : atom) (u : tm),
  y <> x -> upd s x u y = s y.
Proof.
  intros s x y u H. unfold upd. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. congruence.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.

Lemma update_eq : forall (Gamma : context) (x : atom) (T : ty),
  update Gamma x T x = Some T.
Proof. intros. unfold update. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma update_neq : forall (Gamma : context) (x y : atom) (T : ty),
  y <> x -> update Gamma x T y = Gamma y.
Proof.
  intros Gamma x y T H. unfold update. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. congruence.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Lemma sem_ctx_update : forall Gamma sigma x T u,
  sem_ctx Gamma sigma ->
  LR T u ->
  sem_ctx (update Gamma x T) (upd sigma x u).
Proof.

    + rewrite upd_neq; [apply H2 | congruence].
